reached the hospital, and for some time thereafter.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990541–542-0)[\[138\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990541–542) Bose was naked, except for a blanket wrapped around him, and Dr. Yoshimi immediately saw evidence of third-degree burns on many parts of the body, especially on his chest, doubting very much that he would live.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990541–542-1)[\[138\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990541–542) Dr. Yoshimi promptly began to treat Bose and was assisted by Dr. Tsuruta.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990541–542-2)[\[138\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990541–542) According to historian [Leonard A. Gordon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonard_A._Gordon "Leonard A. Gordon"), who interviewed all the hospital personnel later,

> A disinfectant, [Rivamol](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ethacridine_lactate "Ethacridine lactate")\[ _[sic](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sic "Sic")_\], was put over most of his body and then a white ointment was applied and he was bandaged over most of his body. Dr. Yoshimi gave Bose four injections of [Vita Camphor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Camphor#Medicinal_uses "Camphor") and two of [Digitamine](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Digitalis#Cardiac "Digitalis") for his weakened heart. These were given about every 30 minutes. Since his body had lost fluids quickly upon being burnt, he was also given [Ringer solution](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ringer's_solution "Ringer's solution") intravenously. A third doctor, Dr. Ishii gave him a blood transfusion. An orderly, Kazuo Mitsui, an army private, was in the room and several nurses were also assisting. Bose still had a clear head which Dr. Yoshimi found remarkable for someone with such severe injuries.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990542-0)[\[139\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990542)

Soon, in spite of the treatment, Bose went into a coma.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990542-1)[\[139\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990542)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTELebra2008a196–197-7)[\[135\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTELebra2008a196–197) A few hours later, between 9 and 10pm (local time) on Saturday, 18 August 1945, Bose died aged 48.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990542-2)[\[139\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990542)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTELebra2008a196–197-8)[\[135\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTELebra2008a196–197)

Bose's body was cremated in the main Taihoku crematorium two days later, 20 August 1945.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990543-0)[\[140\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990543) On 23 August 1945, the Japanese news agency Do Trzei announced the death of Bose and Shidea.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTELebra2008a196–197-9)[\[135\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTELebra2008a196–197) On 7 September a Japanese officer, Lieutenant Tatsuo Hayashida, carried Bose's ashes to Tokyo, and the following morning they were handed to the president of the Tokyo Indian Independence League, Rama Murti.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990544–545-0)[\[141\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990544–545) On 14 September a memorial service was held for Bose in Tokyo and a few days later the ashes were turned over to the priest of the [Renkōji Temple](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Renk%C5%8Dji_Temple "Renkōji Temple") of [Nichiren Buddhism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nichiren_Buddhism "Nichiren Buddhism") in Tokyo.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTELebra2008a197–198-0)[\[142\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTELebra2008a197–198)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990545-0)[\[143\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990545) There they have remained ever since.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990545-1)[\[143\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990545)

Among the INA personnel, there was widespread disbelief, shock, and trauma. Most affected were the young Tamil Indians from Malaya and Singapore, both men and women, who comprised the bulk of the civilians who had enlisted in the INA.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBaylyHarper200721-2)[\[49\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBaylyHarper200721) The professional soldiers in the INA, most of whom were Punjabis, faced an uncertain future, with many fatalistically expecting reprisals from the British.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBaylyHarper200721-3)[\[49\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBaylyHarper200721) In India the Indian National Congress's official line was succinctly expressed in a letter Mohandas Karamchand (Mahatma) Gandhi wrote to [Rajkumari Amrit Kaur](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajkumari_Amrit_Kaur "Rajkumari Amrit Kaur").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBaylyHarper200721-4)[\[49\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBaylyHarper200721) Said Gandhi, "Subhas Bose has died well. He was undoubtedly a patriot, though misguided."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBaylyHarper200721-5)[\[49\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBaylyHarper200721) Many congressmen had not forgiven Bose for quarrelling with Gandhi and for collaborating with what they considered was [Japanese](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan") fascism. The Indian soldiers in the British Indian army, some two and a half million of whom had fought during the [Second World War](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Second_World_War "Second World War"), were conflicted about the INA. Some saw the INA as traitors and wanted them punished; others felt more sympathetic. The British Raj, though never seriously threatened by the INA, tried 300 INA officers for treason in the [INA trials](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INA_trials "INA trials"), but eventually backtracked.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBaylyHarper200721-6)[\[49\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBaylyHarper200721)

## Ideology

See also: [Political views of Subhas Chandra Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Political_views_of_Subhas_Chandra_Bose "Political views of Subhas Chandra Bose")

Subhas Chandra Bose believed that the [Bhagavad Gita](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bhagavad_Gita "Bhagavad Gita") was a great source of inspiration for the struggle against the British.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTENarangoaCribb2003-0)[\[144\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTENarangoaCribb2003) [Swami Vivekananda](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swami_Vivekananda "Swami Vivekananda")'s teachings on universalism, his nationalist thoughts and his emphasis on social service and reform had all inspired Subhas Chandra Bose from his very young days. The fresh interpretation of India's ancient scriptures had appealed immensely to him.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBoseWerthJogAyer1996-0)[\[145\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBoseWerthJogAyer1996) Some scholars think that Hindu spirituality formed an essential part of his political and social thought.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEChaudhuri1987-0)[\[146\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEChaudhuri1987) As historian [Leonard Gordon](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonard_A._Gordon "Leonard A. Gordon") explains "Inner religious explorations continued to be a part of his adult life. This set him apart from the slowly growing number of atheistic socialists and communists who dotted the Indian landscape."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGordon1990-0)[\[147\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990)

Bose first expressed his preference for "a synthesis of what modern Europe calls [socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Socialism "Socialism") and [fascism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fascism "Fascism")" in a 1930 speech in Calcutta.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPasricha200864–65-0)[\[148\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPasricha200864–65)
Bose later criticised Nehru's 1933 statement that there is "no middle road" between [communism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Communism "Communism") and fascism, describing it as "fundamentally wrong". Bose believed communism would not gain ground in India due to its rejection of nationalism and religion and suggested a "synthesis between communism and fascism" could take hold instead.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBose201198-0)[\[149\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEBose201198) In 1944, Bose similarly stated, "Our philosophy should be a synthesis between [National Socialism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Socialism "National Socialism") and communism."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEShanker_Kapoor2017-0)[\[150\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEShanker_Kapoor2017)

### On Hindu-Muslim unity

In his autobiography, An Indian Pilgrim, Bose says that the Muslims have always played an important role in the public life of the country, before and under British rule, and that the distinction between Hindu and Muslim was largely an artificial creation, like Catholic-Protestant controversy in Ireland, in which the then-rulers have had a hand.[&#8202;](#ref-back-AIP-0)[\[151\]](#refnote-AIP) He goes on to explain why it's a misnomer to describe the political order prior to the advent of the British as "Muslim rule" in the following words:

> "History will bear me out when I say that it is a misnomer to talk of Muslim rule when describing the political order in India prior to the advent of the British. Whether we talk of the Moghul Emperors at Delhi, or of the Muslim Kings of Bengal, we shall find that in either case the administration was run by Hindus and Muslims together, many of the prominent Cabinet Ministers and Generals being Hindus. Further, the consolidation of the Moghul Empire in India was effected with the help of Hindu commanders-in-chief. The Commander-in-chief of Nawab Sirajudowla, whom the British fought at Plassey in 1757 and defeated, was a Hindu, and the rebellion of 1857 against the British, in which Hindus and Moslems were found side by side, was fought under the flag of a Muslim, Bahadur Shah."[&#8202;](#ref-back-AIP-1)[\[151\]](#refnote-AIP)

### Authoritarianism

Bose believed that authoritarianism could bring liberation and reconstruction of Indian society.[&#8202;](#ref-back-195-0)[\[152\]](#refnote-195) He expressed admiration for the authoritarian methods which he saw in Italy and Germany during the 1930s; he thought they could be used to build an independent India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESen1999-1)[\[104\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESen1999)

To a large number of Congress leaders, Bose programme shared enough similarities with Japanese fascists.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEStein2010345-0)[\[153\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEStein2010345) After getting marginalised within Congress, Bose chose to embrace fascist regimes as allies against the British and fled India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2012210-2)[\[8\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMetcalfMetcalf2012210)[&#8202;](#ref-back-Louro-bose-0)[\[13\]](#refnote-Louro-bose) Bose believed that India "must have a political system—State—of an authoritarian character," and "a strong central government with dictatorial powers for some years to come".[&#8202;](#ref-back-197-0)[\[154\]](#refnote-197)

Earlier, Bose had clearly expressed his belief that democracy was the best option for India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERoy20047–8-0)[\[155\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERoy20047–8) However, during the war (and possibly as early as the 1930s), Bose seems to have decided that no democratic system could be adequate to overcome India's poverty and social inequalities, and he wrote that a socialist state similar to that of Soviet Russia (which he had also seen and admired) would be needed for the process of national re-building.[&#8202;](#ref-back-200-0)[\[ar\]](#refnote-200) Accordingly, some suggest that Bose's alliance with the Axis during the war was based on more than just pragmatism and that Bose was a militant nationalist, though not a Nazi nor a Fascist, for he supported the empowerment of women, secularism and other [liberal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Classical_liberalism "Classical liberalism") ideas; alternatively, others consider he might have been using populist methods of mobilisation common to many post-colonial leaders.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESen1999-2)[\[104\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESen1999)

### Antisemitism

Since before the beginning of World War II, Bose was opposed to the attempts to grant Jewish refugees asylum in India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEHayes2011165–166-0)[\[157\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEHayes2011165–166)[&#8202;](#ref-back-202-0)[\[158\]](#refnote-202) The great anti-Jewish pogrom called " [the Night of Broken Glass](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kristallnacht "Kristallnacht")" happened on 9 November 1938. In early December, the pro- [Hindu Mahasabha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hindu_Mahasabha "Hindu Mahasabha") journals published articles lending support to German anti-Semitism. This stance brought Hindu Mahasabha into conflict with the Congress which, on 12 December, issued a statement containing references to recent European events. Within the Congress, only Bose opposed this stance of the party. After some months in April 1939, Bose refused to support the party motion that Jews can find refuge in India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECasolari202089–90-0)[\[15\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECasolari202089–90)[&#8202;](#ref-back-combined-antisemetic-0)[\[159\]](#refnote-combined-antisemetic)[&#8202;](#ref-back-204-0)[\[160\]](#refnote-204)[&#8202;](#ref-back-205-0)[\[161\]](#refnote-205)[&#8202;](#ref-back-206-0)[\[162\]](#refnote-206)[&#8202;](#ref-back-207-0)[\[163\]](#refnote-207)

In 1938, Bose had denounced Nazi racial policy and persecution of Jews.[&#8202;](#ref-back-208-0)[\[164\]](#refnote-208) However, in 1942 he had published an article in the journal _Angriff_, where he wrote that Indians were true Aryans and the 'brethren' of the Germans. Bose added that [Swastika](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swastika "Swastika") (symbol of Nazi Germany) was an ancient Indian symbol. Bose urged that anti-Semitism should be part of Indian liberation movement because the Jews assisted the British to exploit Indians.[&#8202;](#ref-back-209-0)[\[165\]](#refnote-209) _[The Jewish Chronicle](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/The_Jewish_Chronicle "The Jewish Chronicle")_ had condemned Bose as "India's anti-Jewish [Quisling](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Quisling "Quisling")" over this article.[&#8202;](#ref-back-210-0)[\[166\]](#refnote-210)

Romain Hayes describes the troubled legacy of Bose with atrocities related to Jews in the following words:

> The most troubling aspect of Bose's presence in Nazi Germany is not military or political but rather ethical. His alliance with the most genocidal regime in history poses serious dilemmas precisely because of his popularity and his having made a lifelong career of fighting the 'good cause'. How did a man who started his political career at the feet of [Gandhi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahatma_Gandhi "Mahatma Gandhi") end up with [Hitler](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hitler "Hitler"), [Mussolini](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mussolini "Mussolini"), and [Tojo](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hideki_Tojo "Hideki Tojo")? Even in the case of Mussolini and Tojo, the gravity of the dilemma pales in comparison to that posed by his association with Hitler and the Nazi leadership. The most disturbing issue, all too often ignored, is that in the many articles, minutes, memorandums, telegrams, letters, plans, and broadcasts Bose left behind in Germany, he did not express the slightest concern or sympathy for the millions who died in the concentration camps. Not one of his Berlin wartime associates or colleagues ever quotes him expressing any indignation. Not even when the horrors of Auschwitz and its satellite camps were exposed to the world upon being liberated by Soviet troops in early 1945, revealing publicly for the first time the genocidal nature of the Nazi regime, did Bose react.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEHayes2011165-0)[\[52\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEHayes2011165)

## Quotes

His most famous quote was "Give me blood and I will give you freedom".[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEKumar2010b-0)[\[167\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEKumar2010b) Another famous quote was _Dilli Chalo_ ("On to Delhi)!" This was the call he used to give the INA armies to motivate them. Another slogan coined by him was "Ittefaq, Etemad, Qurbani" (Urdu for "Unity, Agreement, Sacrifice").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERoy199651ff-0)[\[168\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERoy199651ff)

## Legacy

Bose's defiance of British authority in India made him a hero among many Indians,[&#8202;](#ref-back-Mythic-0)[\[e\]](#refnote-Mythic)[&#8202;](#ref-back-213-0)[\[as\]](#refnote-213)[&#8202;](#ref-back-214-0)[\[at\]](#refnote-214) however his wartime alliances with [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany "Nazi Germany") and [Imperial Japan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Empire_of_Japan "Empire of Japan") left a legacy vexed by authoritarianism, [anti-Semitism](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anti-Semitism "Anti-Semitism"), and [military failure](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Military_incompetence "Military incompetence").[&#8202;](#ref-back-215-0)[\[au\]](#refnote-215)[&#8202;](#ref-back-216-0)[\[169\]](#refnote-216)[&#8202;](#ref-back-combined-military-lead-0)[\[23\]](#refnote-combined-military-lead)[&#8202;](#ref-back-217-0)[\[av\]](#refnote-217)[&#8202;](#ref-back-218-0)[\[aw\]](#refnote-218)

### Memorials

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/a/a3/Subhas_Chandra_Bose_1964_stamp_of_India.jpg/250px-Subhas_Chandra_Bose_1964_stamp_of_India.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Subhas_Chandra_Bose_1964_stamp_of_India.jpg)

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/1/1b/Subhas_Chandra_Bose_1964_stamp_of_India_2.jpg/250px-Subhas_Chandra_Bose_1964_stamp_of_India_2.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:Subhas_Chandra_Bose_1964_stamp_of_India_2.jpg)

Bose on 1964 stamps of India

Bose was featured on the stamps in India from 1964, 1993, 1997, 2001, 2016, 2018 and 2021.[&#8202;](#ref-back-219-0)[\[170\]](#refnote-219) Bose was also featured in ₹2 coins in 1996 and 1997, ₹75 coin in 2018 and ₹125 coin in 2021.[&#8202;](#ref-back-220-0)[\[171\]](#refnote-220)[&#8202;](#ref-back-221-0)[\[172\]](#refnote-221)[&#8202;](#ref-back-222-0)[\[173\]](#refnote-222)

[Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose International Airport](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhas_Chandra_Bose_International_Airport "Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose International Airport") at Kolkata, West Bengal; is named after him, as well as [Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose Gomoh railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhas_Chandra_Bose_Gomoh_railway_station "Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose Gomoh railway station") at Gomoh, Jharkhand and [Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose Itwari Junction railway station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Itwari_Junction_railway_station "Itwari Junction railway station") at Itwari, Nagpur, Maharashtra.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] [Netaji Express](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Express "Netaji Express"), a train runs between [Howrah](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Howrah_railway_station "Howrah railway station"), West Bengal and [Kalka](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kalka "Kalka"), Haryana. [Cuttack Netaji Bus Terminal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Cuttack_Netaji_Bus_Terminal "Cuttack Netaji Bus Terminal") at Cuttack, Odisha. [Netaji Bhavan metro station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Bhavan_metro_station "Netaji Bhavan metro station") and [Netaji metro station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_metro_station "Netaji metro station") at Kolkata, West Bengal and [Netaji Subhash Place metro station](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhash_Place_metro_station "Netaji Subhash Place metro station") at Delhi. [Netaji Subhash Chandra Bose Island](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_Island,_South_Andaman_district "Ross Island, South Andaman district") at Andaman and Nicobar Island. [Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose Setu](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhas_Chandra_Bose_Setu "Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose Setu") (Longest bridge of Odisha) at Cuttack, Odisha. [Netaji Subhash Chandra Bose Road](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhash_Chandra_Bose_Road "Netaji Subhash Chandra Bose Road") at Kolkata, West Bengal. [INA War Museum](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INA_War_Museum "INA War Museum") at Moirang, Manipur. Netaji Gallery and Art Gallery at Atlanta Point, Port Blair, Andaman and Nicobar Islands.\[ _[citation needed](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed "Wikipedia:Citation needed")_\] [Netaji Indoor Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Indoor_Stadium "Netaji Indoor Stadium") at Kolkata, West Bengal, [DDA Netaji Subhash Sports Complex](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/DDA_Netaji_Subhash_Sports_Complex "DDA Netaji Subhash Sports Complex") at Delhi, [Netaji Stadium](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Stadium "Netaji Stadium") at Port Blair, Andaman and Nicobar Island. [Netaji Subhas Open University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhas_Open_University "Netaji Subhas Open University") at Kolkata, West Bengal, [Netaji Subhash University of Technology](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhash_University_of_Technology "Netaji Subhash University of Technology") at Delhi, [Netaji Subhas University](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhas_University "Netaji Subhas University") at Jamshedpur, Jharkhand and many other things in India are named after him. In 2026, the Sonarpur mountaineering team made the first ascent of an unclimbed peak, approximately 6,274 metres high, in the Chang Chenmo region of the Karakoram in eastern Ladakh. In honour of Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose, the peak was named **Netaji Subhas Parvat**.

On 23 August 2007, [Japanese Prime Minister](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Japanese_Prime_Minister "Japanese Prime Minister"), [Shinzō Abe](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shinz%C5%8D_Abe "Shinzō Abe") visited the [Netaji Bhawan](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Bhawan "Netaji Bhawan") in [Kolkata](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Kolkata "Kolkata").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERoche2007-0)[\[174\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERoche2007)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEThe_Hindu2007-0)[\[175\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEThe_Hindu2007) Abe, who is also the recipient of Netaji Award 2022,[&#8202;](#ref-back-225-0)[\[176\]](#refnote-225) said to Bose's family "The Japanese are deeply moved by Bose's strong will to have led the [Indian independence movement](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Indian_independence_movement "Indian independence movement") from British rule. Netaji is a much respected name in Japan."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERoche2007-1)[\[174\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTERoche2007)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEThe_Hindu2007-1)[\[175\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEThe_Hindu2007)

In 2018, the [Government of India](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Government_of_India "Government of India") renamed some islands in the [Andaman and Nicobar Islands](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Andaman_and_Nicobar_Islands "Andaman and Nicobar Islands"): Ross Island, Neil Island, and Havelock Island were renamed [Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose Dweep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ross_Island,_South_Andaman_district "Ross Island, South Andaman district"), [Shaheed Dweep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shaheed_Dweep "Shaheed Dweep"), and [Swaraj Dweep](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Swaraj_Dweep "Swaraj Dweep"), respectively. In 2019, the Government of India inaugurated a museum on Netaji Subhash Chandra Bose and his INA at [Red Fort](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Red_Fort "Red Fort"), New Delhi. In 2021, the Government of India declared 23 January as [Parakram Divas](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Jayanti "Netaji Jayanti") to commemorate the birth anniversary of Subhas Chandra Bose. Political party, [Trinamool Congress](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Trinamool_Congress "Trinamool Congress") and the [All India Forward Bloc](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/All_India_Forward_Bloc "All India Forward Bloc") demanded that the day should be observed as 'Deshprem Divas'.[&#8202;](#ref-back-226-0)[\[177\]](#refnote-226) In 2022, the Government of India inaugurated a [statue of Subhas Chandra Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Statue_of_Subhas_Chandra_Bose "Statue of Subhas Chandra Bose") at [India Gate](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/India_Gate "India Gate"). Also in the same year, the Government of India started an official award [Subhas Chandra Bose Aapda Prabandhan Puraskar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subhas_Chandra_Bose_Aapda_Prabandhan_Puraskar "Subhas Chandra Bose Aapda Prabandhan Puraskar"), for those who do excellent work in disaster management.[&#8202;](#ref-back-227-0)[\[178\]](#refnote-227)[&#8202;](#ref-back-228-0)[\[179\]](#refnote-228)

### In popular media

[![](https://thumb.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/c/c5/The_tableau_of_Andaman_%26_Nicobar_depicting_the_%27100_years_of_Cellular_Jail_%27%2C_passes_through_the_Rajpath_during_the_Republic_Day_Parade_-_2006%2C_in_New_Delhi_on_January_26%2C_2006.jpg/250px-thumbnail.jpg?utm_source=en.wikipedia.org&utm_campaign=parser&utm_content=thumbnail)](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/File:The_tableau_of_Andaman_&_Nicobar_depicting_the_'100_years_of_Cellular_Jail_',_passes_through_the_Rajpath_during_the_Republic_Day_Parade_-_2006,_in_New_Delhi_on_January_26,_2006.jpg) Bose on the tableau of Andaman & Nicobar in the 2006 [Republic Day Parade](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Republic_Day_Parade "Republic Day Parade") in New Delhi

- _Netaji Subhash_, a feature documentary film about Bose was released in 1947, it was directed by Chhotubhai Desai.[&#8202;](#ref-back-film-0)[\[180\]](#refnote-film)
- _[Subhas Chandra](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Subhas_Chandra "Subhas Chandra")_ is a 1966 Indian [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language "Bengali language")-language biographical film, directed by Pijush Basu.[&#8202;](#ref-back-230-0)[\[181\]](#refnote-230)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEDas_Gupta2015-0)[\[182\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEDas_Gupta2015)
- _[Netaji Subhash Chandra Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhash_Chandra_Bose_(film) "Netaji Subhash Chandra Bose (film)")_ is a 1966 Indian biographical drama film about Bose by [Hemen Gupta](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Hemen_Gupta "Hemen Gupta").[&#8202;](#ref-back-film-1)[\[180\]](#refnote-film)
- In 2004, [Shyam Benegal](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Shyam_Benegal "Shyam Benegal") directed the biographical film, _[Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose: The Forgotten Hero](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_Subhas_Chandra_Bose:_The_Forgotten_Hero "Netaji Subhas Chandra Bose: The Forgotten Hero")_ depicting his life in [Nazi Germany](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nazi_Germany "Nazi Germany") (1941–1943), in Japanese-occupied Asia (1943–1945) and the events leading to the formation of [Azad Hind Fauj](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Azad_Hind_Fauj "Azad Hind Fauj").[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTESalam2005-0)[\[183\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTESalam2005) The film received critical acclaim at the [BFI London Film Festival](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/BFI_London_Film_Festival "BFI London Film Festival"), and has garnered the [National Film Award for Best Feature Film on National Integration](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Nargis_Dutt_Award_for_Best_Feature_Film_on_National_Integration "Nargis Dutt Award for Best Feature Film on National Integration"), and the [National Film Award for Best Production Design](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/National_Film_Award_for_Best_Art_Direction "National Film Award for Best Art Direction") for that year.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEPandohar2005-0)[\[184\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEPandohar2005)[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEThe_Guardian2005-0)[\[185\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEThe_Guardian2005)
- [_Mahanayak_](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mahanayak_(novel) "Mahanayak (novel)"), a 2005 published [Marathi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Marathi_language "Marathi language") historical novel on the life of Subhash Chandra Bose, written by Marathi author [Vishvas Patil](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Vishvas_Patil "Vishvas Patil").
- _[His Majesty's Opponent](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/His_Majesty's_Opponent "His Majesty's Opponent")_, a biography of Subhash Chandra Bose, written by [Sugata Bose](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Sugata_Bose "Sugata Bose"), published in 2011.
- _Subhash Chandra Bose: The Mystery_, a 2016 documentary film by Iqbal Malhotra, follows conspiracy theories regarding Bose's death.[&#8202;](#ref-back-235-0)[\[186\]](#refnote-235)
- _Netaji Bose – The Lost Treasure_ is a 2017 television documentary film which aired on [History TV18](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/History_TV18 "History TV18"), it explores the [INA treasure controversy](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/INA_treasure_controversy "INA treasure controversy").[&#8202;](#ref-back-HistoryTV18-0)[\[187\]](#refnote-HistoryTV18)
- In 2017, [ALTBalaji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ALTBalaji "ALTBalaji") and BIG Synergy Media, released a 9-episode web series, _[Bose: Dead/Alive](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bose:_Dead/Alive "Bose: Dead/Alive")_, created by [Ekta Kapoor](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ekta_Kapoor "Ekta Kapoor"), a dramatised version of the book _India's Biggest Cover-up_ written by [Anuj Dhar](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Anuj_Dhar "Anuj Dhar"), which starred [Bollywood](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bollywood "Bollywood") actor [Rajkummar Rao](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Rajkummar_Rao "Rajkummar Rao") as Subhas Chandra Bose and Anna Ador as [Emilie Schenkl](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Emilie_Schenkl "Emilie Schenkl"). The series was praised by both audience and critics, for its plot, performance and production design.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEGauri2017-0)[\[188\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGauri2017)
- In January 2019 [Zee Bangla](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Zee_Bangla "Zee Bangla") started broadcasting the daily television series _[Netaji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Netaji_(TV_series) "Netaji (TV series)")_.
- _[Gumnaami](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Gumnaami "Gumnaami")_ is a 2019 Indian [Bengali](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Bengali_language "Bengali language") [mystery film](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Mystery_film "Mystery film") directed by [Srijit Mukherji](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Srijit_Mukherji "Srijit Mukherji"), which deals with Netaji's death mystery, based on the [Mukherjee Commission Hearings](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Death_of_Subhas_Chandra_Bose#Mukherjee_Commission_2005 "Death of Subhas Chandra Bose").[&#8202;](#ref-back-238-0)[\[189\]](#refnote-238)

Bose continued to lobby for complete transfer, but did not succeed."[\[3\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEGordon1990502–503)

[&#8202;](#refnote-24)[↑](#ref-back-24-0)Not all Indians, even within the Congress, agreed with the anti-fascist position of the CFD (Congress Foreign Department), and no foreign policy initiative went without contestation. Most prominent was Subhas Chandra Bose, a left-leaning Congressmen from Bengal who famously aligned with and raised an Indian army to support the Axis powers in the Second World War.[&#8202;](#ref-back-Louro-bose-1)[\[13\]](#refnote-Louro-bose)

[&#8202;](#refnote-disturbing)[Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-disturbing-0) [2](#ref-back-disturbing-1)"The most troubling aspect of Bose's presence in Nazi Germany is not military or political but rather ethical. His alliance with the most genocidal regime in history poses serious dilemmas precisely because of his popularity and his having made a lifelong career of fighting the 'good cause'. How did a man who started his political career at the feet of Gandhi end up with Hitler, Mussolini, and Tojo? Even in the case of Mussolini and Tojo, the gravity of the dilemma pales in comparison to that posed by his association with Hitler and the Nazi leadership. The most disturbing issue, all too often ignored, is that in the many articles, minutes, memorandums, telegrams, letters, plans, and broadcasts Bose left behind in Germany, he did not express the slightest concern or sympathy for the millions who died in the concentration camps. Not one of his Berlin wartime associates or colleagues ever quotes him expressing any indignation. Not even when the horrors of Auschwitz and its satellite camps were exposed to the world upon being liberated by Soviet troops in early 1945, revealing publicly for the first time the genocidal nature of the Nazi regime, did Bose react."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEHayes2011165-1)[\[52\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEHayes2011165)
14. [&#8202;](#refnote-29)[↑](#ref-back-29-0)In early December, pro-Hindu Mahasabha journals published articles in favour of German anti-Semitism. This stance brought the Hindu Mahasabha into conflict with the Congress which, on 12th December, made a statement containing clear references to recent European events. Within the Congress, only Bose opposed the party stance. A few months later, in April 1939, he refused to support the party motion that Jews might find refuge in India.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTECasolari202089–90-1)[\[15\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTECasolari202089–90)
15. [&#8202;](#refnote-31)[↑](#ref-back-31-0)The draft resolution read: 'The Committee sees no objection to the employment in India of such Jewish refugees as are experts and specialists and who can fit in with the new order in India and accept Indian standards.' It was, however, rejected by the then Congress President Subhas Chandra Bose, who four years later in 1942 was reported by the _Jewish Chronicle_ of London as having published an article in _Angriff_, a journal of Goebbels, saying that "anti-Semitism should become part of the Indian liberation movement because Jews had helped the British to exploit Indians (21 August 1942)" Although by then Bose had left the Congress, he continued to command a strong influence within the party.[&#8202;](#ref-back-afreedi-2021-bose-0)[\[16\]](#refnote-afreedi-2021-bose)
16. [&#8202;](#refnote-33)[↑](#ref-back-33-0)He had opposed Nehru in permitting political asylum to Jews fleeing Europe in 1939. 23. [&#8202;](#refnote-48)[↑](#ref-back-48-0)"Another small, but immediate, issue for the civilians in Berlin and the soldiers in training was how to address Subhas Bose. Vyas has given his view of how the term was adopted: 'one of our \[soldier\] boys came forward with "Hamare Neta". We improved upon it: "Netaji"...It must be mentioned, that Subhas Bose strongly disapproved of it. He began to yield only when he saw our military group... firmly went on calling him "Netaji"'. (Alexander) Werth also mentioned adoption of 'Netaji' and observed accurately, that it '...combined a sense both of affection and honour...' It was not meant to echo ' [Fuehrer](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Fuehrer "Fuehrer")' or ' [Duce](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Duce "Duce")', but to give Subhas Bose a special Indian form of reverence and this term has been universally adopted by Indians everywhere in speaking about him."

[&#8202;](#refnote-121)[↑](#ref-back-121-0)"Despite any whimsy in implementation, the clarity of Gandhi's political vision and the skill with which he carried the reforms in 1920 provided the foundation for what was to follow: twenty-five years of stewardship over the freedom movement. He knew the hazards to be negotiated. The British must be brought to a point where they would abdicate their rule without terrible destruction, thus assuring that freedom was not an empty achievement. To accomplish this he had to devise means of a moral sort, able to inspire the disciplined participation of millions of Indians, and equal to compelling the British to grant freedom, if not willingly, at least with resignation. Gandhi found his means in non-violent [satyagraha](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Satyagraha "Satyagraha"). He insisted that it was not a cowardly form of resistance; rather, it required the most determined kind of courage.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEStein2010297-0)[\[82\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEStein2010297)

[&#8202;](#refnote-215)[↑](#ref-back-215-0)(p.117) the INA was raised during the Second World War, with the support of the Imperial Japanese Army (IJA); lasted less than three years; and went through two different configurations during that period. In total, it numbered some 40,000 men and women, half of whom are estimated to have been recruited from Indian Army prisoners of war (POWs). The INA's battlefield performance was quite poor when assessed either alongside the IJA or against the reformed Fourteenth Army on the battlefields of Assam and Burma. Reports of its creation in 1942/3 caused consternation among the political and military leadership (p. 118) of the GOI, but in the end its formation did not constitute a legitimate mutiny, and its presence had a negligible impact on the Indian Army.[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMarston2014117–118-1)[\[20\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMarston2014117–118)
48. [&#8202;](#refnote-217)[↑](#ref-back-217-0)"The (Japanese) Fifteenth Army, commanded by... Maj.-General Mutuguchi Renya consisted of three experienced infantry divisions—15th, 31st and 33rd—totalling 100,000 combat troops, with the 7,000 strong 1st Indian National Army (INA) Division in support. It was hoped the latter would subvert the Indian Army's loyalty and precipitate a popular rising in British India, but in reality the campaign revealed that it was largely a paper tiger."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMoreman2013124–125-1)[\[24\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMoreman2013124–125)
49. [&#8202;](#refnote-218)[↑](#ref-back-218-0)"The real fault, however, must attach to the Japanese commander-in-chief Kawabe. Even more incredibly, he still hoped for great things from Bose and the INA, despite all the evidence that both were busted flushes."[&#8202;](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEMcLynn2011429-1)[\[25\]](#refnote-FOOTNOTEMcLynn2011429)

     - Huff, Gregg (2020), [_World War II and Southeast Asia: Economy and Society under Japanese Occupation_](https://books.google.com/books?id=waECEAAAQBAJ&pg=PR26), Cambridge University Press, p.xvi, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-1-107-09933-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-107-09933-3 "Special:BookSources/978-1-107-09933-3"), [LCCN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCCN_(identifier) "LCCN (identifier)") [2020022973](https://lccn.loc.gov/2020022973), [archived](https://web.archive.org/web/20230712062303/https://books.google.com/books?id=waECEAAAQBAJ&pg=PR26) from the original on 12 July 2023, retrieved 28 January 2022, Chronology of World War II in the Pacific: **18 August 1945** Subhas Chandra Bose killed in a plane crash in Taiwan.
     - Satoshi, Nakano (2012), _Japan's Colonial Moment in Southeast Asia 1942–1945: The Occupiers' Experience_, London and New York: Routledge, p.211, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-1-138-54128-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-138-54128-3 "Special:BookSources/978-1-138-54128-3"), [LCCN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCCN_(identifier) "LCCN (identifier)") [2018026197](https://lccn.loc.gov/2018026197), 18 August 1945. Upon hearing of Japan's defeat in the Pacific War, Chandra Bose, who had dedicated his life to the anti-British Indian independence struggle, immediately decided to head for the Soviet Union, "out of my commitment to ally with any country that regards the US and Britain as their enemies." The Japanese Foreign Ministry and the military cooperated in Bose's exile, placing him aboard a Japanese plane headed for Dalian (Yunnan) from Saigon to put him in touch with the Soviet army. After a stopover in Taipei, however, the passenger plane crashed immediately after takeoff. Despite freeing himself from the wreckage, Bose was engulfed in flames and breathed his last.
     - Blackburn, Kevin; Hack, Karl (2012), _War Memory and the Making of Modern Malaysia and Singapore_, Singapore: NUS Press, National University of Singapore, p.185, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-9971-69-599-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-9971-69-599-6 "Special:BookSources/978-9971-69-599-6"), Even before the INA memorial was completed, it became the focus of mourning for Singapore's Indian community. The cause of this premature use was news that Bose had died in a plane crash at Taipei, on 18 August. He had been trying to escape capture after the surrender of Japan on 15 August. Singapore and Malaya remained under Japanese control until 5 September when British forces returned. On 26 August 1945, meanwhile, wreaths were laid at the INA memorial in honour of Bose. A large group gathered at the memorial and speeches on Bose's life were made by Major-General M.Z. Kiani and Major-General S.C. Alagappan of the INA, and ITL members. The Japanese newspaper, the Syonan Shimbun, reported that "during the ceremony which lacked nothing in solemnity and dignity, many husky warriors—Sikhs, Punjabis, and others from the Central Provinces—soldiers who had taken part in the actual war operations were seen to shed tears as they saluted for the last time a giant portrait of Netaji which occupied a prominent position in front of the War Memorial".

My fate is with them. But as the Japanese plane took off from Taipei airport its engines faltered and then failed. Bose was badly burned in the crash. According to several witnesses, he died on 18 August in a Japanese military hospital, talking to the very last of India's freedom. British and Indian commissions later established convincingly that Bose had died in Taiwan. These were legendary and apocalyptic times, however. Having witnessed the first Indian leader to fight against the British since the great mutiny of 1857, many in both Southeast Asia and India refused to accept the loss of their hero. Rumours that Bose had survived and was waiting to come out of hiding and begin the final struggle for independence were rampant by the end of 1945.

[Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-gordon-bose-death-0) [2](#ref-back-gordon-bose-death-1) [3](#ref-back-gordon-bose-death-2)[Gordon, Leonard A.](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Leonard_A._Gordon "Leonard A. Gordon") (1990), _Brothers Against the Raj: A Biography of Indian Nationalists Sarat and Subhas Chandra Bose_, New York and Oxford: Columbia University Press, pp.539–542, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [0-231-07443-3](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-231-07443-3 "Special:BookSources/0-231-07443-3"), On the plane were: Bose, Shidei, Rahman. Also: Lt. Col. Tadeo Sakai; Lt. Col. Shiro Nonogaki; Major Taro Kono; Major Ihaho Takahashi, Capt. Keikichi Arai, an air force engineer; chief pilot Major Takizawa; co-pilot W/O Ayoagi; navigator Sergeant Okishta; radio-operator NCO Tominaga. The crew was in the front of the aircraft and the passengers were wedged in behind ... there were no proper seats on this aircraft. The plane finally took off (from Saigon) between 5:00 and 5:30 pm on August 17. Since they were so late in starting, the pilot decided to land for the night at Tourane, Vietnam. ... The take-off from Tourane at about 5:00 am was normal ... and they flew to Taipei (Japanese: Taihoku) ...At Taipei ... the crew and passengers took their places ... and they were ready to go at 2:30. ... Just as they left the ground—barely thirty meters up and near the edge of the airfield—there was a loud noise. ... With an enormous crash they hit the ground. ... The injured, including Bose and Rahman and the surviving Japanese officers, were taken to Nanmon Army Hospital. Ground personnel at the airfield had already called the hospital shortly before 3:00 pm and notified Dr. Taneyoshi Yoshimi, the surgeon in charge of the hospital, to prepare to receive the injured. ... Upon arrival the doctor noticed that Bose ... had third degree burns all over his body, but they were worst on his chest. ...Bose and Rahman were quickly taken to the treatment room and the doctor started working on Bose, the much more critically injured man. Dr Yoshimi was assisted by Dr. Tsuruta. ... An orderly, Kazuo Mitsui, an army private, was also in the room, and several nurses were also assisting. ... Bose's condition worsened as the evening darkened. His heart grew weaker. Finally between 9.00 and 10.00 pm, Bose succumbed to his terrible burns.

019. [↑](#ref-back-cronin-0)Cronin, Joseph (2025). "Affiliations, Entanglements, and 'Otherness': The Experiences of German-Speaking Jewish Refugees in India, 1938–1948". In Cho, Joanne Miyang; Kurlander, Eric; McGechin, Douglas (eds.). _German-Speaking Jewish Refugees in Asia, 1930–1950: Shelter from the Storm?_. London and New York: Routledge. [doi](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Doi_(identifier) "Doi (identifier)"): [10.4324/9781003517795-18](https://doi.org/10.4324%2F9781003517795-18). Bose's views on the Nazis' main victims, the Jews, and specifically on the Jewish refugees, were also ambiguous. He wrote to his wife in 1937: "The Jews in Europe have attained so many positions because they are very skilful and the Aryans are very stupid \[dumm\] - otherwise, how could the foreigners \[sic\] in Europe make such progress?" Bose also accused his Congress colleague Nehru of "seeking to make India an asylum for Jews" in early 1939, knowing full well that their number would, at most, amount to a few thousand in a population of three hundred million. So, while Bose's opinions did not stem from a place of deep ideological antisemitism, his partial ignorance of the situation for Jews in Germany and Europe at that time, combined with his political allegiances and priorities, led him to suspect that Jewish refugees being sent to India was just another manifestation of Britain flexing its colonial might, of political power play, rather than a reluctant and insufficient response to a rapidly escalating humanitarian crisis.

[↑](#ref-back-matthews-bose-congress-0)Matthews, Roderick (2021), _Peace, Poverty, and Betrayal: A New History of British India_, Oxford University Press, By this point the Congress leadership was in turmoil after the election of Subhas Chandra Bose as president in 1938. His victory was taken, principally by Bose himself, as proof that Gandhi's star was in decline, and that the Congress could now switch to his personal programme of revolutionary change. He set no store by non-violence and his ideals were pitched a good deal to the left of Gandhi's. His plans also included a large amount of leadership from himself. This autocratic temperament alienated virtually the whole Congress high command, and when he forced himself into the presidency again the next year, the Working Committee revolted. Bose, bitter and broken in health, complained that the 'Rightists' had conspired to bring him down. This was true, but Bose, who seems to have had a talent for misreading situations, seriously overestimated the strength of his support—a significant miscalculation, for it led him to resign in order to create his own faction, the Forward Bloc, modelled on the kind of revolutionary national socialism fashionable across much of Europe at the time.
029. [↑](#ref-back-haithcox-bose-andINC-oldguard-0)Haithcox, John Patrick (1971), _Communism and Nationalism in India: M. N. Roy and Comintern Policy, 1920–1939_, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, pp.282–283, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [0-691-08722-9](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/0-691-08722-9 "Special:BookSources/0-691-08722-9"), [LCCN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCCN_(identifier) "LCCN (identifier)") [79120755](https://lccn.loc.gov/79120755), One of the principal points of dispute between Bose and the Congress high command was the attitude the party should take toward the proposed Indian federation. The 1935 Constitution provided for a union of the princely states with the provinces of British India on a federal basis...Following his election for a second term, Bose charged that some members of the Working Committee were willing to compromise on this issue. Incensed at this allegation, all but three of the fifteen members of the Working Committee resigned. The exception was Nehru, Bose himself, and his brother Sarat. There was no longer any hope for reconciliation between the dissidents and the old guard.

[Woermann](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Ernst_Woermann "Ernst Woermann") recommended the indefinite postponing of any announcement of Bose's presence in Germany and cautioned the Foreign Minister Joachim von Ribbentrop that the time had not yet come to recognise Bose's government in-exile. 151. [Jump up to: 1](#ref-back-AIP-0) [2](#ref-back-AIP-1)Subhas Chandra Bose (1965). [_An Indian Pilgrim (An Unfinished Autobiography And Collected Letters 1897-1921)_](https://archive.org/details/indianpilgrimunf00subh/page/12/mode/1up). pp.12–13.
152. [↑](#ref-back-195-0)Deka, Meeta (2013), _Women's Agency and Social Change: Assam and Beyond_, SAGE Studies on India's North East, New Delhi and Thousand Oaks, CA: SAGE Publications, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-81-321-1138-2](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-81-321-1138-2 "Special:BookSources/978-81-321-1138-2"), (pp. 134–135) Bose was convinced that his ideology could bring about the liberation of India and a total reconstruction of Indian society along authoritarian-socialist lines, envisaging gender equality therein. As mayor of Calcutta, he believed that his policy and programme was a synthesis of socialism and fascism, on the lines of modern Europe. In the early 1930s, he stated, 'We have here the justice, the equality, the love, which is the basis of Socialism as it stands in Europe today.' In the late 1930s, he reiterated his belief in the efficacy of authoritarian government and a synthesis of fascism and socialism, while in 1944 when addressing the students at Tokyo University, he asserted that India must have a political system 'of an authoritarian character ... \[and\] our philosophy should be a synthesis between National Socialism and Communism'.
153. [↑](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEStein2010345-0)[Stein 2010](#CITEREFStein2010), pp.345.
154. [↑](#ref-back-197-0)Harrison, Selig S. (1960), _India: The Most Dangerous Decades_, Princeton Legacy Library, Princeton, NJ: Princeton University Press, p.314, [LCCN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCCN_(identifier) "LCCN (identifier)") [60005749](https://lccn.loc.gov/60005749), The most categorical and unabashed program for dictatorship in India's political heritage, finally, was laid down by the late Subhas Chandra Bose. He argued that India "must have a political system—State—of an authoritarian character," "a strong central government with dictatorial powers for some years to come," "a government by a strong party bound together by military discipline ... as the only means of holding India together." The next phase in world history, Bose predicted, would produce "a synthesis between Communism and Fascism, and will it be a surprise if that synthesis is produced in India?"
155. [↑](#ref-back-FOOTNOTERoy20047–8-0)[Roy 2004](#CITEREFRoy2004), pp.7–8.
156. [↑](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEBoseBose1997a319–320-0)[Bose & Bose 1997a](#CITEREFBoseBose1997a), pp.319–320.
157. [↑](#ref-back-FOOTNOTEHayes2011165–166-0)[Hayes 2011](#CITEREFHayes2011), pp.165–166.
158. [↑](#ref-back-202-0)Kumaraswamy, P. R. (2020), _Squaring the Circle: Mahatma Gandhi and the Jewish National Home_, Routledge, p.153, In his presidential address, Subhas Chandra Bose highlighted the contradictory nature of the British Empire and its inconsistent policy over Palestine. As a heterogeneous empire, Bose observed, the British had to be pro-Arab in India and pro Jewish elsewhere, and accused that London "has to please Jews because she cannot ignore Jewish high finance. On the other hand, the India Office and Foreign Office have to placate the Arabs because of the Imperial interests in the Near East and India."' While his reasoning was logical, Bose's anti-Jewish slur was no different from the anti-Semitic remarks in the (Muslim) League deliberations referred to earlier. Bose also opposed Nehru's efforts to provide asylum to a limited number of European Jewish refugees who were fleeing from Nazi persecution. Despite the opposition led by Bose, Nehru "was a strong supporter of inviting (Jewish refugees) to settle down in India... (and felt that) this was the only way by which Jews could be saved from the wrath of the Nazis... Between 1933 and the outbreak of the War, Nehru was instrumental in obtaining the entry of several German Jewish refugees into India"
159. [↑](#ref-back-combined-antisemetic-0)Bruckenhaus, Daniel (2017), _Policing Transnational Protest: Liberal Imperialism and the Surveillance of Anticolonialists in Europe, 1905–1945_, Oxford: Oxford University Press, p.213, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-0-19-066001-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-19-066001-7 "Special:BookSources/978-0-19-066001-7"), [LCCN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/LCCN_(identifier) "LCCN (identifier)") [2016042217](https://lccn.loc.gov/2016042217), Epilogue and conclusion: Finally, however, the example of Germany also demonstrates that their work in Europe frequently forced anticolonialists to make difficult moral choices, as their presence in that continent required them to take a position not only on colonialism worldwide, but also on inner-European political and ideological conflicts. This was true, especially, during World War II. The war situation brought to stark light, one last time, the contradictions within the western political model of rule, leading to a rift among the anticolonialists then present in Europe. As the western empires fought against Nazi Germany, most anticolonialists felt that they could no longer support, simultaneously, the emancipatory projects of anticolonialism and antifascism. Some, such as Subhas Chandra Bose, began to cooperate with the radically racist Nazis against colonialism, while others decided to work against Nazism with the very western authorities who had been engaged, over the previous decades, in creating a widespread network of trans-national surveillance against them.
160. [↑](#ref-back-204-0)Roland, Joan G. (2017), _Jewish Communities in India: Identity in a Colonial Era_, Routledge, p.342, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-0-7658-0439-6](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-7658-0439-6 "Special:BookSources/978-0-7658-0439-6"), On 21 August 1942 the Jewish Chronicle of London reported that Bose was anti-Semitic and had published an article in Angriff, the organ of Goebbels, in which he described Indians as the real ancient Aryans and the brethren of the German people. He had said that the swastika was an old Indian sign and that anti-Semitism must become a part of the Indian freedom movement, since the Jews, he alleged, had helped Britain to exploit and oppress the Indians. The Jewish Advocate expressed horror at Bose's statement about a Jewish role in India's exploitation but added, "one may expect anything from one who has traveled the road to Berlin in search of his country's salvation." Norman Shohet pointed out how insignificant a part in the economic and political life of the country the Jews of India actually played. He also mentioned that other Indian leaders had so far not shown any anti-Semitic leanings, but that on the contrary, Gandhi, Nehru, Dr. B. R. Ambedkar, and others had been positively friendly to the Jews.
161. [↑](#ref-back-205-0)Aafreedi, Navras J. (2021), "Holocaust education in India and its challenges", in Aafreedi, Navras J.; Singh, Priya (eds.), _Conceptualizing Mass Violence: Representations, Recollections, and reinterpretatons_, Abington and New York: Routledge, p.154, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-1-00-314613-1](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-00-314613-1 "Special:BookSources/978-1-00-314613-1"), Jawaharlal Nehru called the Jews 'People without a home or nation' and sponsored a resolution in the Congress Working Committee. Although the exact date is not known, yet it can be said that it probably happened in December 1938 at the Wardha session, the one that took place shortly after Nehru returned from Europe. The draft resolution read: 'The Committee sees no objection to the employment in India of such Jewish refugees as are experts and specialists and who can fit in with the new order in India and accept Indian standards.' It was, however, rejected by the then Congress President Subhas Chandra Bose, who four years later in 1942 was reported by the _Jewish Chronicle_ of London as having published an article in _Angriff_, a journal of Goebbels, saying that "anti-Semitism should become part of the Indian liberation movement because Jews had helped the British to exploit Indians (21 August 1942)" Although by then Bose had left the Congress, he continued to command a strong influence within the party.
162. [↑](#ref-back-206-0)Weinberg, Gerhard L. (2011), _A World at Arms: A Global History of World War II_, Cambridge and New York: Cambridge University Press, 2nd Edition, p.xx, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-0-521-61826-7](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-0-521-61826-7 "Special:BookSources/978-0-521-61826-7"), None of the works that deal with ... Subhas Chandra Bose, or his Indian National Army has engaged either Bose's reaction to German mass killing of Sinti and Roma (Gypsies) because their ancestors came from India or the reaction of the soldiers in his army to the sex slaves kidnapped in Japanese-occupied lands and held in enclosures attached to the camps in which they were being trained to follow their Japanese comrades in the occupation of India.
163. [↑](#ref-back-207-0)Shindler, Colin (2010), _Israel and the European Left: Between Solidarity and Deligitimization_, New York: Bloomsbury Publishing, Continuum, p.112, [ISBN](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/ISBN_(identifier) "ISBN (identifier)") [978-1-4411-8898-4](https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Special:BookSources/978-1-4411-8898-4 "Special:BookSources/978-1-4411-8898-4"), Bose requested a declaration from the Germans that they supported the movement for freedom in India—and in Arab countries. He had opposed Nehru in permitting political asylum to Jews fleeing Europe in 1939. He was prepared to ingratiate himself with Nazi ideology by writing for Goebells's _Der Angriff_ in 1942. He argued that anti-Semitism should become a factor in the struggle for Indian freedom since the Jews had collaborated with British imperialism to exploit the country and its inhabitants.
